QUESTION TYPE(S) FOR YOU: Must Be True / Most Supported, and Must Be False

- Optional: if a shortlist has fewer than 10 that deserve to be models, you may look beyond it. C:\Users\Joshu\AppData\Local\Temp\claude\C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os\d175f546-f515-4224-a5ed-1afb844b5971\scratchpad\lib\scores.json has every complete explanation with its family ("fam") and score; print any one in full with: python C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\system\scripts\lr-explanations\show.py PT123-S1-Q5 (run from C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os).

RUBRIC (score each pick 0/1/2 on each, total /12):
P Prediction: Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices.
X Precision: names the exact inference/gap/relationship in this passage's words.
E Eliminations: every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason (no verdict-only notes); the most popular wrong answer (highest student %) gets the most attention and says why it tempts.
T Transfer: states the move so it works on the next question of this type (a test, a rule, a recurring trap).
C Clarity: plain, no padding, no pasted repetition across notes.
L Learner value: teaches the move (why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy).
DISQUALIFY or flag: a choice note that misquotes or describes a different answer choice than the one printed; imported outside facts used to justify the key; mocking the question or answer; heavy em-dash formulaic register ("This answer captures..."); a correct-answer note that argues for the wrong key; transcription damage that makes it unreadable (e.g. diagrams flattened into nonsense). CHECK FIDELITY: compare each note you rely on against the printed answer choice text.

WHAT TO PICK: 10 to 20 per question type (at least 10 if 10 deserve it; if fewer are genuinely top-tier, say so and give the number). Spread across difficulty (easy 1-2, medium 3, hard 4-5) and across sub-patterns (for MBT/MSS: conditional chains, contrapositive triggers, quantifier/"most" inferences, math/percent, causal difference-maker, pivots, logical completion/fill-in; for MBF: contradicting a conditional, contradicting an inference, least compatible / most justifiably rejected). Prefer variety.

OUTPUT: write your result as markdown to C:\Users\Joshu\AppData\Local\Temp\claude\C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os\d175f546-f515-4224-a5ed-1afb844b5971\scratchpad\lib\picks-MBT-MBF.md with, per question type:

3. "What the weaker candidates got wrong": 3 to 5 bullets with slot examples.
4. Fidelity problems found in any candidate (slot + what), even ones you didn't pick.
Then reply with a short summary. Do not edit any file other than your picks file. Do not write to any database. Plain language; no em dashes.

44	
45	| PTs | Explained | Avg words | Complete | Em-dashes per 1k words | Notes |
46	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
47	| 101-122 | 1,158 | 553 | 85% | 0.5 | Full LSAT Lab voice. Many golden picks come from here. |
48	| 123-138 | 1,025 | 362 | 51% | 0.7 | Noticeably thinner. Most "incomplete" rows have a breakdown but no Goal step (346), and choice notes are often one or two lines. Some notes read like generic summaries (PT126-S4-Q20: "This introduces arbitrators, which are outside the scope"). |
49	| 139-156 | 1,097 | 588 | 91% | 0.6 | Same voice as 101-122. Consistently strong. |

51	
52	**The PT157/158 explicit-Standard register.** Choice notes run about 100+ words each, open with formulas ("This answer captures...", "This answer identifies the argument's central flaw"), use em-dashes at roughly ten times the rate of everything else, and repeat the same point several ways. More importantly, they contain accuracy slips I did not find in the single-style text at the same rate:
53	- PT158-S3-Q2: the D note describes a different answer ("can effectively combine knowledge") from the actual choice ("can effectively solve many problems"); A is tagged "No Impact" while its note argues it undermines the argument.
54	- PT158-S2-Q16: the correct-answer note imports outside history (Plato's family wealth, Philip of Macedon) to justify the answer.
55	- PT158-S2-Q18: the A note argues against a claim the choice does not make.
56	
57	That is why no explicit-Standard item made the golden set, and why the house voice rules (no em-dashes) matter for the writing guide.
58	
59	**Two voices inside the single style.** Most text is one conversational author (voice A: "Hey, author", "Let's look for", FABS, Curious Fact, Given that / How can it be that). A second, more formal author appears in about 15% of questions (voice B: "Beware of answers that...", "If you chose this answer...", "This answer choice..."). Voice B is accurate and structured but lighter on prediction and analogy. Examples: PT146-S2-Q8, PT151-S2-Q1, PT152-S4-Q5, PT154-S1-Q10, PT155-S4-Q1. Both voices can produce a strong explanation; voice A produces most of the best ones.

61	## 3. What separates strong from loose (the empirical comparison)
62	
63	I picked ten explanations that were clearly strong and ten that were complete but loose, read all twenty side by side, then measured a few things.
64	
65	- **Strong (10):** PT139-S4-Q18 Flaw, PT142-S4-Q22 Flaw, PT145-S2-Q19 Paradox, PT154-S1-Q18 NA, PT153-S2-Q13 Weaken, PT146-S2-Q13 Strengthen, PT147-S1-Q17 Evaluate, PT152-S2-Q21 Principle-Conform, PT150-S3-Q15 Agree/Disagree, PT144-S3-Q17 Role.
66	- **Loose but complete (10), with the failure each shows:** PT127-S2-Q3 Weaken (two wrong answers get one line each: "This supports the argument's explanation"); PT126-S4-Q20 Weaken (generic summary notes, a Goal that restates the task); PT124-S3-Q10 Role (short, and "Haha. This answer is unthinkably weird"); PT151-S2-Q18 Strengthen (117 words total; the 29% trap gets one sentence); PT146-S2-Q8 Flaw (every wrong answer is "The argument never discusses X", correct note is one line); PT142-S4-Q5 Principle (mechanical, no reason why the trap tempts); PT155-S2-Q10 Paradox (fine idea, thin notes); PT158-S3-Q2 Strengthen (long, but misquotes a choice); PT158-S2-Q16 Paradox (long, but brings in outside facts); PT148-S1-Q20 Method (sound analysis, but calls the correct answer "so, so dumb").
67	
68	Measured averages (strong vs loose):
69	
70	| Measure | Strong | Loose |
71	|---|---|---|
72	| Total words | 773 | 394 |
73	| Words in the Evaluate step | 169 | 77 |
74	| Words in the Goal step | 66 | 43 |
75	| Goal names 3+ content words from the stimulus | 0.9 | 0.5 |
76	| Avg words per wrong-answer note | 87 | 45 |
77	| Wrong-answer notes under 20 words (verdict only) | 0.0 per question | 1.1 per question |
78	| Wrong-answer notes that run an explicit test (negate it, does it weaken, does it trigger the rule, why should we believe it) | 1.4 of 4 | 0.2 of 4 |
79	| Words in the correct-answer note | 143 | 60 |
80	| Examples, analogies or worked numbers | 2.0 | 0.5 |
81	| Stated generalizations ("whenever", "on X questions", "usually") | 1.6 | 0.3 |
82	
83	What actually separates them, in order of how reliably it predicted my judgment:
84	
85	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument.** Strong ones do real work before the choices: they build the objection ("Given that X, how could we argue not-Y?"), do the arithmetic (25% of $400 vs 13% of $1,000), or set the two populations side by side. Loose ones restate the stimulus and move on.
86	2. **The Goal names the answer, not the task.** Strong: "look for an answer saying the fish in the leafless trees are exposed to predators that hunt by sight." Loose: "Find the flaw that mistakes a response to danger for a cause", "Identify an answer choice that supports fairness." Strong Goals often give two acceptable shapes (a linking answer or a defender).
87	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason tied to this question, and usually a test.** The loose group's signature is the verdict-only note: "This is irrelevant.", "That is unnecessary.", "The argument never discusses profits." Strong notes say what the answer claims, what was needed, and where they diverge, often by running the family's test on it (negate it; would it weaken; is the trigger established; does the conclusion match the rule's right side).
88	4. **The popular trap gets the most attention.** Strong explanations spend their longest wrong-answer note on the choice that drew 15-30% of students and say why it tempts. Loose ones treat it like every other wrong answer.
89	5. **The correct-answer note explains the fit, and often generalizes it.** Strong: "Another correct answer performing the same function could have said..." (PT146-S2-Q13); the karaoke/Jenga reason why A → C still works when you predicted A and B → C (PT139-S4-Q21). Loose: "This is the flaw."
90	6. **Fidelity.** Two loose items are long. Length did not save them: a misquoted answer choice, imported outside facts, or the author disowning the question destroys model value regardless of word count.
91	
92	What did not separate them: word count by itself (PT152-S4-Q19 is 479 words and strong; PT158-S2-Q16 is 716 and loose), the choice-tag vocabulary, and the presence of jokes (several strong ones joke; it only hurts when the joke is at the question's or the answer's expense).
93

95	
96	Gate (hard requirement): full Argument breakdown and all five choice notes. Every shortlisted candidate passed.
97	
98	Scored 0 / 1 / 2:
99	
100	| Criterion | 2 | 1 | 0 |
101	|---|---|---|---|
102	| **P** Prediction | Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices (may give two acceptable shapes) | Goal names the task or admits it cannot predict | No prediction |
103	| **X** Precision | Names the exact gap, flaw or relationship in this argument's words | Uses the right label but stays generic | Label only, or wrong |
104	| **E** Eliminations | Every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason; the popular trap gets the most attention | Most do; one or two verdict-only notes | Verdict-only notes |
105	| **T** Transfer | States the move in a form that works on the next question of this type (the test, the rule, the recurring trap) | Transfer is implicit | None |
106	| **C** Clarity | Plain, no padding, no pasted repetition | Some padding or pasted notes | Hard to follow |
107	| **L** Learner value | Teaches what a student needs to understand and repeat the move: why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy, a second correct answer that would also work | Justifies the key well but does not teach the move | Only justifies the key |
108	
109	Plus a **fidelity flag** (not scored; disqualifying if serious): `slip` factual or typo error, `tone` the author mocks the question or answer, `register` explicit-Standard PT157/158 prose, `transcription` damaged text.
110	
111	Learner value is the criterion that most often separated a 10 from a 12. Many explanations justify the key correctly (1) but only the best ones also show the student the move they will reuse (2).
112	
113	## 5. Step structure per family (DB, most common sequences)
114	
115	Labels normalized (parenthetical indicator words dropped: "Conclusion (therefore)" counts as Conclusion).
116	
117	- **Flaw (524):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (67%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (9%); with an Intermediate Conclusion step (5%). Evaluate usually names the famous flaw or builds the objection; Goal often gives both the formal and the "fails to consider" phrasing.
118	- **Must Be True / Most Supported (462):** Statements > Evaluate > Goal (45%); Statements only (16%). Statements is often a diagram. Evaluate scans for conditional, causal, math and pivot wording and names the inference to make.
119	- **Necessary Assumption (352):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (66%). Goal typically offers a linking answer and a defender answer, and says "negate it".
120	- **Weaken (335) / Strengthen (277):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (59% / 55%). Evaluate uses "Given that ... how could we argue that ..." or Curious Fact / Alternate Explanation / Plausibility.
121	- **Paradox (213):** "Apparent Paradox > Evaluate" (22%, older tests) or "Given that ... > How can it be that / Why is it that ... > Evaluate > Goal" (about 30% combined, often with Evaluate / Goal merged).
122	- **Sufficient Assumption (193):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (53%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (21%). Goal usually diagrams the missing link as the logic path.
123	- **Principle-Strengthen (181):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (48%). Goal is "If evidence, then conclusion" in general terms.
124	- **Principle-Conform (99):** mixed. Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (24%) when the principle is in the answers; Principle > Evaluate > Goal (9%) or Principle > Goal (7%) when it is in the stimulus.
125	- **Main Conclusion (144) / Role (126):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (42% / 53%), with an Intermediate Conclusion step in about 8%. Evaluate almost always opens with the reminder that the conclusion usually comes early (first sentence or after a pivot).
126	- **Parallel (100) / Parallel Flaw (123):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (49% / 67%), Evidence first in about 19-35%. Goal is an algebraic recipe (P1, P2, C) or a plain-language template.
127	- **Agree/Disagree (119):** speaker-named steps. "Speaker's Claims > Speaker's Responses > Goal", often a numbered claim list with "Does [speaker 2] disagree?" per claim.
128	- **Method (91):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (37%) for one speaker; "X's Conclusion > X's Evidence > Y's Response > Evaluate > Goal" for two speakers.
129	- **Evaluate (28):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (75%).
130	
131	## 6. Recurring phrasing conventions
132	
133	- **Frames:** "Given that ... how could we (still) argue that ..." (Weaken, Strengthen, NA, Flaw); "Curious Fact ... Author's Explanation ... Alternate Explanation / Plausibility" (any causal argument); "Given that ... How can it be that ..." (Paradox); "If Evidence, then Conclusion" (Principle, SA).
134	- **Tests in choice notes:** negate it and ask if it weakens (NA); "fails to consider X" means ask whether X weakens, "presumes X" means ask whether X is necessary (Flaw); "argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence" (Method, Flaw); a rule can only prove its right-hand side (Principle, SA); "can we support the Agree position and the Disagree position?" (Agree/Disagree); "Why should we believe this?" (Role, Main Conclusion).
135	- **Recurring trap names:** Too Strong (the most common NA and MBT tag), Out of Scope, No Impact, Unclear Impact, No Distinction (Paradox), Deepens Paradox, Wrong Flaw / Not an Objection (Flaw), Bad Conclusion / Evidence / Trigger Match (Principle, Parallel), Premise / Last-Claim Trap / Opposing Position (Main Conclusion), Unsupported Agree/Disagree Position (Agree/Disagree), Fake Opposite / Illegal Negation (NA), Reversal (SA). Tags come from the local markdown; the dump does not include them.
136	- **Rules stated as rules:** "Most X can only be concluded from a Most X premise"; "some / sometimes / can is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox, SA"; "the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence"; "about 85-90% of Main Conclusion questions put the conclusion in the first sentence or after a pivot"; "on EXCEPT, three of the working answers usually work one way and one works another".
137	- **Voice habits that should not be copied:** mocking the question or answer ("hot garbage", "so, so dumb", "unthinkably weird", "dumpster fire"), and the PT157/158 em-dash register.
138	
139	## 7. `Explanations/targeted-questions/`
140	
141	Three files: a README and two explanations, PT134-S1-Q16 (Flaw, belief vs implication, answer A) and PT158-S4-Q24 (Weaken, food-supply chain, answer D). The README says they were "added for LSAT U use when Joshua needs specific questions available outside a full PrepTest-section explanation file."
142	
143	They are not LSAT Lab transcriptions. The layout is different and the parser will not read it: an H1 title, a metadata block (type, answer, LSAT U order, bank file), H2 sections for Stimulus, Prompt, Argument breakdown, **Written explanation** (a free prose section the LSAT Lab files do not have), Answer choice explanations with an italic tag, and a closing **Student takeaway**. The prose is written in LSAT U's own voice: no em-dashes, short sentences, jokes built on the stimulus ("the drug sneaks into the food supply wearing a tiny trench coat", "Still a raccoon in the kitchen"), and a one-line takeaway ("Break the link that matters. That's the move.").
144	
145	Authorship: git attributes them to `vnjkoxpl-42905` (Joshua's account) inside a bulk commit dated 2026-09-05 titled "Update RRC launch page copy", with no co-author line and no other reference in the repo or wiki. The style, the house-voice compliance and the README wording point to an AI assistant (Claude or Codex) drafting them for Joshua. That is an inference; the git history cannot confirm who typed them.
146	
147	They are useful as a picture of the target voice (especially "Written explanation" and "Student takeaway", which have no LSAT Lab equivalent). They are not in the golden set because they are outside the corpus and use a different structure.
148	
149	## 8. Caveats
150	
151	- All scores are one reader's judgment, formed by reading each candidate once. Treat 11 vs 12 as a tie.
152	- Features and difficulty come from the question bank. 110 explained questions have no bank match, and some shortlisted items have no features in the bank at all (PT143-S1-Q6, PT154-S1-Q10), so the coverage grid undercounts them.
153	- I read about 125 explanations in full or near-full, weighted toward the highest-scoring candidates in each family and difficulty band. I did not read all 3,000, so a better exemplar for a thin cell may exist.

17	## What separates a top-tier explanation from a loose one
18	
19	Length does not. These do:
20	
21	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument** ("How could we argue the opposite?") instead of restating it.
22	2. **The Goal names the answer** it expects, not just the task.
23	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason and usually a test.** Loose ones average one verdict-only note per question ("This is irrelevant").
24	4. **The most popular trap gets the most attention.**
25	5. **The correct-answer note teaches the move**, so a student can repeat it on the next question, not just confirm the key.
26	
27	Complete (full breakdown plus all five Choice Notes) is a gate, not a score.

4	=================== PT127-S1-Q26 | diff 3 | key E | score 24.6 | features: -
5	STIMULUS: Essayist: Common sense, which is always progressing, is nothing but a collection of theories that have been tested over time and found useful. When alternative theories that prove even more useful are developed, they gradually take the place of theories already embodied in common sense. This causes common sense to progress, but, because it absorbs new theories slowly, it always contains some obsolete theories.
6	STEM: If all of the essayist’s statements are true, then which one of the following must be true?
7	(A) [6% | Too Strong: never be absorbed] At least some new theories that have not yet been found to be more useful than any theory currently part of common sense will never be absorbed into the body of common sense.
8	(B) [10% | Too Strong: generally] Of the useful theories within the body of common sense, the older ones are generally less useful than the newer ones.
9	(C) [5% | Unsupported Causal Relationship] The frequency with which new theories are generated prevents their rapid absorption into the body of common sense.
10	(D) [8% | Too Strong: each theory] Each theory within the body of common sense is eventually replaced with a new theory that is more useful.
11	(E) [71% | Correct] At least some theories that have been tested over time and found useful are less useful than some other theories that have not been fully absorbed into the body of common sense.
12	--- BREAKDOWN
13	## Statements
14	*conditionals* (always, nothing but)
15	
16	Common → Always progressing
17	sense
18	
19	Common → collection of time-tested
20	sense theories found useful
21	
22	*conditional* (when)
23	
24	Alternate theory eventually
25	more useful than → supplant theories
26	common sense in common sense
27	
28	*causal* (causes, because)
29	
30	The process of newer, common
31	more useful theories ⇢ sense
32	eventually supplanting progresses
33	theories in common sense
34	
35	Common sense absorbs Common sense
36	new theories slowly ⇢ always has some
37	obsolete stuff
38	## Evaluate
39	We're very attracted to Conditional or Causal indicator wording, when we're doing Must Be True and Most Supported. And that's because often conditional as well as causal statements **chain** together. If A leads to B, and B leads to C, they can test us on our understanding that A leads to C.
40	
41	But then other times we get a random mess of stuff, like this problem. Diagramming these statements is probably not going to help us, since they don't really chain together. And since there are a few conditional ideas and a couple causal ones, should we anticipate a conditional chain or a causal chain?
42	
43	In short, we don't know. However, the final sentence does have a **pivot** (but / yet / however). Usually, when an Inference paragraph *Ends with a Pivot*, the correct answer deals with the tension created by that pivot idea.
44	
45	What does it tell us, if anything, that because common sense absorbs new theories slowly, it always contains some obsolete theories?
46	
47	"Obsolete" means that something is no longer widely used or believed, or its functionality has been superseded. The iPod and iPhones made listening to music on CD's obsolete.
48	
49	So if some theories in common sense are obsolete, it's because alternative theories have already proven themselves more useful. I guess common sense is like a big corporation. Even when smaller businesses innovate better ways, the big corporation's pace of change is glacial, so it has some outmoded ways of doing things.
50	## Goal
51	Let's look for anything provable, possibly dealing with the tension created by the fact that common sense always contains some obsolete ideas.
52	
53	We know three things about "common sense"
54	- always progressing
55	- a collection of theories that have been tested over time and found useful
56	- always contains some obsolete theories
57	
58	Maybe the correct answer will speak to an overlap between some of those traits.
59	--- CHOICE NOTES
60	(A) We don't have any sort of rule in this paragraph that gives us a way to prove that something "will not be absorbed".
61	We only have a rule that says,
62	"If alt. theory proves more useful, will be absorbed (eventually)"
63	(B) Can we really say most of the older theories in common sense are less useful than most of the newer ones?
64	Nothing in the paragraph let's us quantify a comparison in usefulness.
65	The temptation in this answer is that a new theory in common sense occurs because that new theory is more useful than the one it's replacing.
66	But let's say that there are 100 theories in common sense. There might be like 65 ironclad theories that are super useful. It's unlikely an alternate theory that's even more useful will ever be developed.
67	Meanwhile, there are 35 decent theories that are somewhat useful. But over time, we keep finding ways to improve on them. They get swapped out for somewhat more useful alternatives.
68	But that doesn't mean that the average usefulness of those 35 (the newer ones) will be higher than the usefulness of the 65 older theories, that are so useful, they've never been replaced!
69	(C) The passage told us that superior theories are absorbed into common sense slowly.
70	This answer seems to be positing some causal backstory to explain why they're absorbed slowly (it's saying "there are just so many new theories that common sense doesn't want to overreact, so it takes it slow"?)
71	We are just told that common sense absorbs new theories slowly. The passage doesn't explain why it absorbs new theories slowly, so we can't prove this causal claim about why it does so.
72	(D) Nothing in the paragraph guarantees that every theory in common sense will eventually be replaced.
73	The only trigger we know that guarantees that a theory will be replaced is when an alternate theory is found that proves more useful.
74	Do we know that, for every theory in common sense, there will eventually be an alternate theory that is found to be more useful?
75	No, we weren't told that, which is why this answer is too strong.
76	(E) As we suspected, this answer deals with the tension created by the ending Pivot idea.
77	Since common sense always contains some obsolete theories, that means that there are always some alternate theories out there that are superior to some of the theories in common sense.
78	"Superior" means that there are always some theories in the world that are more useful than some theories still embodied in common sense.
79	We know that all theories embodied in common sense have been tested over time and found useful. So we add up claims to derive this answer:
80	1. CS always contains some obsolete theories (some theories that are less useful than some alternate theory out there in the world)
81	+
82	2. all the theories in CS have been proven useful
83	∴ Some theories in CS are theories that have proven useful, but are nevertheless less useful than alternate theories that haven't yet been absorbed (since CS absorbs new theories slowly).
84	We anticipated that the correct answer might try to forge some overlap between some pairing of the three things we knew about "common sense", and this answer is combining the 2nd and 3rd:
85	- always progressing
86	- a collection of theories that have been tested over time and found useful
87	- always contains some obsolete theories
88	It's saying that "some of the theories in common sense have been tested over time and found useful but are also obsolete".

92	STIMULUS: In an experiment, subjects were shown a series of images on a computer screen, appearing usually at the top but occasionally at the bottom. Subjects were asked to guess each time where the next image would appear on the screen. They guessed correctly less than half of the time. The subjects all reported that they based their guesses on patterns they believed they saw in the sequence. Instead, if they had simply guessed that the next image would always appear at the top, they would have been correct most of the time.
93	STEM: If all of the statements above are true, which one of the following must also be true?
94	(A) [19% | Unsupported Relationship] If the subjects had always guessed that the next image would appear at the top, they would not have been basing their guesses on any pattern they believed they saw in the sequence.
95	(B) [9% | Too Broad / Strong] Basing one's guesses about what will happen next on the basis of patterns one believes one sees is less likely to lead to correct guesses than always guessing that what has happened before will happen next.
96	(C) [6% | Too Strong: no pattern] There was no predictable pattern that one could reasonably believe occurred in the series of images on the computer screen.
97	(D) [56% | Correct] Some of the subjects sometimes guessed that the next image would appear at the bottom of the computer screen, but were incorrect.
98	(E) [10% | Out of Scope: rational / Too Strong: most rational] The most rational strategy for guessing correctly where the next image would appear would have been simply to always guess that the image would appear at the top.
99	--- BREAKDOWN
100	## Statements
101	People where shown images that usually appeared at the top of the screen but sometimes appeared at the bottom. After each appearance, they were asked to guess where the next image would appear. They were wrong most of the time. All of them said they were basing their guess on a pattern they believed they saw. If they had simply guessed that next image would always appear at the top, they would have been correct most of the time.
102	## Evaluate
103	This is a tough one to anticipate. We're usually looking for Conditional / Causal / Quantitative language as a clue for where the answer choice inference may come from, but this paragraph is long and messy. There's a counterfactual conditional in the final sentence, saying that "if they had simply guessed next image will be on top, then they would have been correct most of the time". We know they were basing their guess on a pattern they thought they perceived, so apparently they didn't perceive a pattern of next image is always on top.
104	## Goal
105	Because of how long this paragraph is, with five sentences, we should probably just re-read the paragraph one more time for good understanding and then use the answers to guide us. If an answer seems somewhat provable, then we'll research the details in the paragraph.
106	--- CHOICE NOTES
107	(A) Let's stop at the trigger and ask ourselves if we know anything ...
108	were there any people that always guessed the next image would appear at the top?
109	
110	We don't really know. We know that most of them didn't guess that, since they were wrong most of the time. And the final sentence is giving us a counterfactual about what would have been true if they had all always guessed "next image will be on top".
111	
112	So given that we don't know anything about this trigger, it's hard to say we can derive a must be true of what would follow.
113	
114	If we assume that nobody was constantly guessing "next image on top", then this answer is just talking about a counterfactual.
115	
116	It's saying, "Yeah, nobody guessed X, but if they had guessed X, it wouldn't have been based on any pattern they believed they saw."
117	
118	How would we prove that counterfactual?
119	
120	How can we say in a hypothetical situation what someone would / wouldn't have believed they saw in the sequence?
121	
122	The contrapositive of this conditional rule is saying:
123	```
124	if they were basing their guesses on a pattern they believed they saw
125	  → they would not have always guessed next image on top
126	```
127	We know for a fact that all of the subjects were guessing based on a pattern they believed they saw.
128	
129	So according to this answer choice, none of them would have guessed "next image will appear on top".
130	
131	That's too strong. We know most of them weren't guessing "next image will appear on top" because most of the time they were wrong, but it's still possible that some of the subjects guessed "next image will appear on top".
132	
133	It's possible that some subjects guessed perfectly. The low accuracy of all the other subjects could lower the group's accuracy to where it's still true to say "the subjects guessed correctly less than half of the time".
134	
135	(as a desperation move, you might avoid guessing this tempting answer because Must Be True questions often put a very tempting but wrong answer non-coincidentally into the slot for choice (A). Given that this answer was conditional strength and in a famously trappy spot, we might convince ourselves we're safer choosing something farther down in the answer choices that also makes sense)
136	(B) This answer is too strong because it's presented as a truism that goes beyond this experiment.
137	
138	We would only be able to comment on what sort of guessing would have had what sort of accuracy for this experiment. We can't extrapolate a rule that's applicable to other situations.
139	(C) It's possible there was a predictable pattern someone could have reasonably believed occurred.
140	
141	Maybe it looked like it was doing "3 tops, 1 bottom, 3 tops, 1 bottom".
142	
143	Even if it didn't end up doing that exact pattern, it might have been close enough to that for us to say there was a predictable pattern that one could reasonably believe occurred.
144	
145	The fact that these people thought they saw patterns, but were largely wrong, doesn't make those patterns unreasonable ones to believe you saw.
146	(D) This is weird, but at least appealing because of how safe/soft the language is. Can we prove that at least once in this experiment, some guessed "next image will be at the bottom" and ended up being wrong?
147	
148	It sure sounds very very plausible. After all, we know that they were mostly wrong and that the images were mostly occurring at the top of the screen, so you'd assume that there was at least one time where one of the wrong guesses was "next image will be bottom."
149	
150	In order for this weak statement to be false, we'd have to show that it's possible that people were never wrong when they guessed "bottom". All of their wrong answers came from guessing "top" and being wrong.
151	
152	We can prove this statement using Most + Most quantifier logic. Whenever we have these two ideas:
153	Most A's are B
154	Most A's are C
155	we can infer Some B are C
156	
157	We know that
158	Most images appeared at the top
159	Most images were incorrectly guessed
160	
161	So we can infer that
162	"some incorrect guesses were images that appeared at the top".
163	
164	We know that most images appeared at the top because the first sentence says that images usually (i.e. more than 50% of the time) were on top.
165	
166	We know that most guesses were incorrect, because "they guessed correctly less than half of the time", so they guessed incorrectly more than half of the time.
167	
168	Thus, (and nauseatingly), we can derive that at least one of those wrong guesses had to occur when the image appeared at the top of the screen.
169	
170	In order to have a wrong guess about an image that appears at the top of the screen, it must be that the person guessed bottom of the screen.
171	(E) Just because the subjects turned out to be wrong most of the time doesn't mean they were necessarily behaving irrationally. They thought they perceived a pattern. Making a guess based on a perceived pattern is pretty rational. We call that inductive reasoning.

177	STIMULUS: Most cats like to go outside to play when the temperature is above freezing. However, Jamil does not allow his cat to go outside unless at least one member of his family is outside, and Jamil’s family members go outside only when the sun is shining or it is hot outside.
178	STEM: Which one of the following can be properly inferred from the information above?
179	(A) [5% | Bad Trigger / Outcome Match] If Jamil’s cat is outside, then the temperature is above freezing.
180	(B) [3% | Illegal Negation] If Jamil’s cat is not outside, it is cloudy and it is not hot outside.
181	(C) [7% | Illegal Reversal] If the sun is shining or it is hot outside, Jamil’s cat is probably outside.
182	(D) [6% | Illegal Reversal] If at least one member of Jamil’s family is outside, then Jamil’s cat is outside also.
183	(E) [78% | Correct] If the sun is not shining and it is not hot outside, Jamil’s cat is not outside.
184	--- BREAKDOWN
185	## Information
186	```
187	quantifier (most)
188	Most cats = like to go out and play when temp > freezing
189	conditional (unless = if not)
190	If there is not at least one   →   cat not allowed
191	member outside                     to go out
192	conditional (only when = right side)
193	family members   →   sun is shining
194	go outside           or hot outside
195	```
196	## Evaluate
197	Whenever we see a conditional idea in an Inference stimulus, we make it the center of our attention look for two possible moves:
198	  1. can we chain it up with other conditionals (if any)?
199	  2. can we apply it to any provided facts (may have to contrapose)?
200	  Since we noticed two conditional indicators, "unless" and "only when", we should start by figuring out whether they chain together. We may have to contrapose our conditionals to see where they would line up. They need some overlapping claim, and in this case the overlapping idea is whether or not Jamil's family members are outside.
201	  The easiest way to chain stuff together mentally is to use "X requires Y" language. Any conditional, X → Y, can be read as "X requires Y".
202	  In order for the cat to be allowed outside, there must be a family member outside. And in order for there to be a family member outside, it has to be sunning or hot (or both).
203	  We can write that chain of requirements, if we think it will help us maintain a clear image of the chain.
204	```
205	Cat allowed   →   some family members   →   sun's shining
206	outside           are outside               or hot outside
207	```
208	  What about the first sentence, the Most claim? Can we apply this rule to that sentence? Are there any overlapping ideas?
209	  Not really. The concept of "likes to go outside and play" is not the same as "whether the cat is allowed to go outside". And the concept of "when the temperature is above freezing" isn't a good match for ideas like "Sun's shining / hot outside".
210	  The temperature could be above freezing whether or not the sun is shining, and whether or not it's hot outside.
211	  We could say that "if hot outside, then temp is above freezing, and thus most cats would like to go outside and play". But it would be pretty awkward to add that on to the chain, because it would build off the "hot outside" half of our Or outcome.
212	  It's probably more likely that the first sentence just isn't intended to combine with the 2nd and 3rd claims.
213	## Goal
214	Look for an answer rewarding us for having assembled that chain of requirements, but pick any answer we can prove from the provided information.
215	--- CHOICE NOTES
216	(A) We don't have any knowledge triggered by "If J's cat is outside". We know that "If J's cat is allowed outside, then some family members are out there, which means it's sunny, hot, or both."
217	
218	```
219	Cat allowed   →   some family members   →   sun's shining
220	outside           are outside               or hot outside
221	```
222	
223	But the cat might be outside without being allowed outside.
224	
225	And even if we played along and equated those, we just know that it's sunny, hot, or both. We can't prove that the temperature is above freezing.
226	
227	The sun can be shining, even when the temperature is freezing or colder.
228	(B) We don't have any knowledge triggered by "If J's cat is not outside". We know that "If J's cat is allowed outside, then some family members are out there, which means it's sunny, hot, or both."
229	
230	```
231	Cat allowed   →   some family members   →   sun's shining
232	outside           are outside               or hot outside
233	```
234	
235	This answer is trying to flip the lightswitch on all these ideas, which is illegal.
236	
237	It's thinking,
238	
239	```
240	Cat not allowed   →   no family members   →   sun's not shine
241	outside               are outside             or not hot outside
242	```
243	(C) We don't have any knowledge triggered by "If sun is shining or hot outside". That's an outcome, not a trigger.
244	
245	```
246	Cat allowed   →   some family members   →   sun's shining
247	outside           are outside               or hot outside
248	```
249	
250	This answer is trying to read backwards, which is illegal.
251	
252	It's thinking,
253	
254	```
255	Cat allowed   ←   some family members   ←   sun's shining
256	outside           are outside               or hot outside
257	```
258	(D) We do have some knowledge triggered by "If some family members are outside". That tells us that it's sunny, hot, or both.
259	
260	```
261	Cat allowed   →   some family members   →   sun's shining
262	outside           are outside               or hot outside
263	```
264	
265	This answer is trying to read backwards, which is illegal.
266	
267	It's thinking,
268	
269	```
270	Cat allowed   ←   some family members   ←   sun's shining
271	outside           are outside               or hot outside
272	```
273	(E) This provides the contrapositive of the chain we derived.
274	
275	```
276	Cat allowed   →   some family members   →   sun's shining
277	outside           are outside               or hot outside
278	```
279	
280	When we contrapose
281	A → B → C or D
282	we get
283	~D and ~C → ~B → ~A
284	
285	So the contrapositive of our chain is
286	
287	```
288	~sun shining      →   no fam members   →   cat not
289	and ~hot outside      outside              allowed outside
290	```
291	
292	That's what this answer is reflecting, "If this 1st thing, then this 3rd thing".
293	
294	The answer does add on an assumption that "if the cat is not allowed outside, then the cat is not outside". That is definitely not something we were told, and it's not a common sense truth that "whenever cats aren't allowed outside, they are not outside", because we all know cats escape sometimes.

395	=================== PT107-S1-Q6 | diff 3 | key E | score 23.0 | features: Medical, Volume Dial
396	STIMULUS: These days, drug companies and health professionals alike are focusing their attention on cholesterol in the blood. The more cholesterol we have in our blood, the higher the risk that we shall die of a heart attack. The issue is pertinent since heart disease kills more North Americans every year than any other single cause. At least three factors—smoking, drinking, and exercise— can each influence levels of cholesterol in the blood.
397	STEM: Which one of the following can be properly concluded from the passage?
398	(A) [13% | Relative vs. Absolute One-Claim Trap] If a person has low blood cholesterol, then that person’s risk of fatal heart disease is low.
399	(B) [1% | Too Strong: as great a risk Out of Scope: moderate vs. heavy] Smoking in moderation can entail as great a risk of fatal heart disease as does heavy smoking.
400	(C) [4% | Out of Scope: high-cholesterol diet] A high-cholesterol diet is the principal cause of death in North America.
401	(D) [2% | Too Strong: the only way] The only way that smoking increases one’s risk of fatal heart disease is by influencing the levels of cholesterol in the blood.
402	(E) [81% | Correct] The risk of fatal heart disease can be altered by certain changes in lifestyle.
403	--- BREAKDOWN
404	## Information
405	Drugs companies and health professionals are looking at blood cholesterol.
406	Volume Dial relationship (more x, more y) The more cholesterol in our blood, the higher the risk that we die of a heart attack.
407	Strong Quantified Claim (#1 cause of death) Heart disease kills more North Americans every year than any other single cause.
408	Causal (can influence) Smoking, drinking, and exercise can each influence levels of cholesterol in the blood.
409	## Evaluate
410	We have a couple Overlapping Ideas: blood cholesterol, fatal heart disease
411	We might be able to derive some synthesis by combining those ideas. For example, combining the first and last sentence, we could say something like, "Drug companies and health professionals are focusing on [something that can be influenced by smoking / drinking / exercise]."
412	Or maybe we could say something like, "The #1 cause of death is tightly correlated with [something that can be influenced by smoking / drinking / exercise".
413	## Goal
414	Look for the most provable claim. Be wary of answers that seem Too Strong/Specific, Unknown Comparisons, or Unmentioned ideas.
415	The correct answer will probably pull together two or more ideas by using the overlapping concepts of "blood cholesterol" or "heart disease".
416	--- CHOICE NOTES
417	(A) This sounds sort of like the 2nd sentence, which should make us scared to pick it. The correct answer on Inference questions almost never just re-states one claim. Instead, it is almost always derived by combining two or more claims.
418	
419	The 2nd sentence is interpreted in a Relative way. You might be a regular long-distance runner in excellent health with a low risk of heart disease. If you start increasing the cholesterol in your blood, you will increase your risk of heart disease, but that doesn't mean you're suddenly at a high risk (in Absolute terms).
420	
421	Similarly, someone who smoke and drank liquor all their lives and doesn't exercise would have a very high risk of heart disease. If they reduce the cholesterol in their blood down to a low level, that would lower their risk of fatal heart disease, but it wouldn't mean that their risk of fatal heart disease is low in absolute terms.
422	
423	As an analogy, we could say, "the less stuff you put on the roof of your vehicle, the lower its overhead clearance is when driving through tunnels".
424	
425	But that doesn't mean, "If a vehicle has little on top of its roof, its overhead clearance is low." After all, it could be a really tall vehicle to begin with.
426	
427	In both LR and RC, if the passage uses the Relative version of a word and an answer uses the Absolute version of that word, it will almost always be wrong (and vice versa).
428	(B) This presents an Unknown Comparison between moderate smoking and heavy smoking and counterintuitively says that moderate smoking is just as risky as heavy smoking, when it comes to fatal heart disease.
429	
430	We can't support that strongly worded comparison at all. If anything, we would think that a heavier smoker would have higher blood cholesterol and thus higher risk of fatal heart disease.
431	(C) The passage never talks about high-cholesterol diets at all. We know that heart disease is the principal cause of death in North America, and that cholesterol in the blood is heavily linked to heart disease.
432	
433	But we don't know if a high cholesterol diet is usually the reason for people having high cholesterol in their blood.
434	(D) Just because "raising blood cholesterol" is the only way mentioned in this paragraph doesn't mean we can support the idea that smoking only impacts your risk of heart disease by impacting your blood cholesterol level.
435	
436	Maybe smoking also makes your lung functioning worse, so your heart has to work harder than it should to oxygenate your blood, which over time increases your risk of heart disease.
437	(E) The soft language of "can be altered" is the primary reason why we'd try to fight for this answer choice.
438	
439	Did the passage mention certain lifestyle habits?
440	
441	Hmm, would we call smoking / drinking / exercise components of one's lifestyle? Sure, that seems fair. If we stopped smoking or stopped drinking or started exercising, that would seem to qualify as "a change in lifestyle".
442	
443	Could that alter our risk of fatal heart disease?
444	
445	Probably. After all, we know that these three factors influence blood cholesterol and blood cholesterol is tightly linked (Volume Dial relationships are the strongest possible form of correlation) to fatal heart disease / heart attacks.
446	
447	The Volume Dial relationship is not inherently causal. We could say, "the more birthdays you've had, the less of your lifetime remains" (depressing!)
448	
449	That doesn't mean that "having birthdays" is causing you to "have less life left". Both things are being caused by the unstoppable arrow of time.
450	
451	So why is LSAT cool with treating this Volume Dial as causal? 1) Partly, it seems to be just a little sloppy. This question stem should say Most Supported, and then it would be fine.
452	
453	2) The first and third sentences contribute some context that makes it seem really likely that the relationship between blood cholesterol and heart disease is causal.
454	
455	The first sentence is saying that drug companies and health professionals are focusing on cholesterol in the blood (so it's implied that it would have some effect on health). The third sentence says, "the issue [of blood cholesterol's close link to heart disease] is pertinent since heart disease is the #1 killer".
456	
457	We probably wouldn't say that the connection between blood cholesterol and heart disease is pertinent unless we thought it was a causal connection.
458	
459	3) Whether or not it's a causal connection, it's still given to us as a fact that "the less cholesterol you have in your blood, the lower your risk of fatal heart attack is". So if we know that changes to smoking / drinking / exercise could effect changes to your blood cholesterol, then according to the second sentence there would also be a change to our risk of fatal heart attack.

462	=================== PT148-S1-Q22 | diff 4 | key D | score 23.0 | features: Math-y Stuff, Quantifiers
463	STIMULUS: Most of the new cars that Regis Motors sold last year were purchased by residents of Blomenville. Regis Motors sold more new cars last year than it did in any previous year. Still, most new cars purchased by Blomenville residents last year were not purchased from Regis Motors.
464	STEM: If the statements above are true, which one of the following must also be true?
465	(A) [7% | Out of Scope: record # of new to Blomenville's] Regis Motors sold more new cars to residents of Blomenville last year than they had in any previous year.
466	(B) [5% | Out of Scope: record # bought by Blomenville's] The total number of new cars purchased by residents of Blomenville was greater last year than it was in any previous year.
467	(C) [19% | Out of Scope: other than Regis Motors] A car retailer other than Regis Motors sold the most new cars to residents of Blomenville last year.
468	(D) [67% | Correct] The number of new cars purchased last year by residents of Blomenville is greater than the number of new cars sold by Regis Motors.
469	(E) [4% | Out of Scope: market share] Regis Motors’ share of the new car market in Blomenville last year increased over its share the year before.
470	--- BREAKDOWN
471	## Statements
472	  ```
473	  quantifiers (most)
474	  Most new cars sold   were purchased by
475	  Regis Motors         residents of
476	  last year            Blomenville
477	  Most new             purchased by   were not sold
478	  residents of                        by
479	  Blomenville                         Regis Motors
480	  math-y comparison
481	  Regis Motors sold more new cars last year than in any previous year.
482	  ```
483	## Evaluate
484	On Must Be True questions, we're primarily scanning for Conditional, Quantified, or Causal ideas.
485	  When we see Quantifiers, we know there are a couple different types of quantity overlap / relationship inferences that LSAT likes to test:
486	  ```
487	  All A's are B          Most A's are B
488	  Some A's are C         Most A's are C
489	  -------------------    -------------------
490	  infer: some B's are C  some B's are C
491	  ```
492	  Since we were given two Most claims, we should be thinking about that second one, the "Most + Most" inference. However, for that inference to work, we need both Most claims to be about the same group.
493	  This question is actually testing a more obscure "Most + Most" inference, that has only shown up a couple times (both tests are in the 70s / 80s).
494	  ```
495	  Most A's are B
496	  Most B's are ~A
497	  -------------------
498	  infer: more B's than A's
499	  ```
500	  Take any two qualities, say "likes cake" and "likes ballet".
501	  Most people who like ballet like cake, but
502	  most people who like cake do not like ballet.
503	  Let's pretend we're in some ballroom that contains people who just like ballet, just like cake, like both, or like neither.
504	  Let's pretend we have 15 people who like ballet.
505	  Most of them like cake, so a minimum of 8.
506	  So we'll say 15 people who like ballet,
507	  8 of them like cake, 7 of them don't.
508	  Now let's think about the second fact,
509	  "Most people who like cake don't like ballet",
510	  What's the minimum number of cake lovers?
511	  We already know there are 8 people who like cake and ballet.
512	  So 8 of the people who like cake also like ballet. But we know most of the people who like cake do not like ballet, so there would have to be at least 9 of them.
513	  Thus the minimum number of cake lovers in 17.
514	  We've proven that when we hear
515	  Most people who like ballet like cake, but
516	  most people who like cake do not like ballet.

649	Our reading / thinking goal on Must Be True and Most Supported (i.e. Inference questions) is to see whether we can combine or synthesize any of the claims they've provided in order to derive some other true claim. To do this, we pay attention to whether we're given any Conditional rules, Causal relationships, or Math-y facts. But the expression neither / nor is banning both things, not one or the other.

Although just a small fraction of the classes at Harrison are night classes, 38 percent of Harrison students take at least one night class. That figure is lower for Harrison students living in Westerville: Only 29 percent of those students take at least one night class.
1427	STEM: If the statements above are true, which one of the following must also be true?
1428	(A) [46% | Correct] More than 38 percent of the students at Harrison who live in Pulham take a night class.
1429	(B) [25% | Too Strong: more than 50%] More than 50 percent of the students who take night classes at Harrison are from Pulham.
1430	(C) [10% | Out of Scope Comparison: # vs. %] More students at Harrison live in Westerville than live in Pulham.

1433	--- BREAKDOWN
1434	## Facts
1435	All students live in Pulham or Westerville.
1436	  38% of students take a night class.
1437	  29% of students in W take a night class.
1438	## Evaluate
1439	We primarily read Inference questions looking for ways to combine ideas using Conditional, Causal, Comparative, or Math-y thinking. This one definitely seems math-y.
1440	  We can infer that more than 38% of Pulham's students a night class, since the average of Pulham and Westerville comes out to be 38%. If W is below that number, than Pulham has to be above that number.
1441	  Suppose all students at a school or girls or boys. If 70% of the boys play soccer and 80% of the girls play soccer, what % of the students at the school play soccer?

1618	=================== PT153-S2-Q5 | diff 2 | key A | score 20.9 | features: Causal Difference-Maker
1619	STIMULUS: One theory concerning the importance of vitamin C in the human diet holds that vitamin C plays a crucial role in the production and maintenance of the body’s supply of collagen, an important protein occurring almost exclusively in connective tissue and bones. For this reason, some doctors believe that vitamin C can be useful in treating the symptoms of some common illnesses.
1620	STEM: Which one of the following is most strongly supported by the information above?
1621	(A) [84% | Correct] Some doctors believe that there are illnesses that affect the state of connective tissue or bones.
1622	(B) [1% | Too Strong: the only] Some doctors believe that vitamin C is the only substance that produces and maintains collagen.
1623	(C) [5% | Unsupported Causal Relationship] Some doctors believe that strengthening connective tissue and bones increases the body’s ability to use certain vitamins.
1624	(D) [5% | Too Strong: most effective] Some doctors believe that use of vitamin C is the most effective treatment for certain common illnesses.
1625	(E) [6% | Too Strong: any illness] Some doctors believe that any illness that can be ameliorated with vitamin C causes deterioration of connective tissue and bones.
1626	--- BREAKDOWN
1627	## Information
1628	One theory: vitamin C plays a crucial role in producing and maintaining a supply of collagen, a protein found almost only in connective tissue and bones.
1629	## causal (for this reason)
1630	Doctors think vitamin C can be useful in treating the symptoms of some common illnesses.
1631	## Evaluate
1632	Our job on these questions is to combine claims in order to derive some synthesis, and we primarily look for clues based on Causal, Comparative, Pivot or Conditional wording. The Causal Connector "for this reason" is giving us a big hint that this question wants us to spell out the logic of this causal connection. If I said, "Kim is a huge fan of Lebron James. For this reason, she is going to hate what I just tweeted", what can we infer from the connection between the first thing and the second thing? The most reasonable suspicion is that, "I just tweeted something that portrays Lebron James in a negative light". At a minimum, we can supportably infer, "I just tweeted something that relates to Lebron James." In this example, "Vitamin C plays a crucial role in making / maintaining the body's supply of collagen. For this reason, doctors think Vitamin C can be useful in treating symptoms of some common illnesses." "Vitamin C" is the Overlapping Ingredient in both claims. The way that a synthesis usually works is that we remove the overlap and combine the one-offs. Combining A is B with B is C gives us A is C. The synthesis removes the overlapping B and ties together the one-offs of A and C. Combining Kim likes Lebron with so, Kim won't like my tweet gives us my tweet relates to Lebron. The synthesis removes the overlapping Kim and ties together the other two ideas.
1633	## Goal
1634	Combining Vitamin C helps us with collagen with so, Vitamin C could help with symptoms gives us symptoms relate to collagen. Let's look for something like "the symptoms of some common illnesses relate to our supply of collagen / our supply of an important protein occurring mainly in connective tissue and bones".

1644	(C) The passage is suggesting that taking vitamin C would have an effect on our connective tissue and bones.
1645	
1646	This answer is saying that our connective tissue and bones would have an effect on our ability to take vitamin C.
1647	(D) All we heard is "can be useful in treating the symptoms of some common illnesses". We have no support for the way more extreme claim that vitamin C is "the #1 most effective treatment for certain common illnesses".

1724	=================== PT145-S4-Q13 | diff 2 | key D | score 20.8 | features: Math-y Stuff, Quantifiers
1725	STIMULUS: Most people who are skilled banjo players are also skilled guitar players. But most people who are skilled guitar players are not skilled banjo players.
1726	STEM: If the statements above are true, which one of the following must also be true?
1727	(A) [6% | Contradicted] There are more people who are skilled at playing both the guitar and the banjo than there are people who are skilled at playing only one of the two instruments.
1728	(B) [4% | Out of Scope: more likely to succeed] A person trying to learn how to play the guitar is more likely to succeed in doing so than is a person trying to learn how to play the banjo.
1729	(C) [1% | Unsupported Comparison: more skill] Playing the guitar takes more skill than playing the banjo does.
1730	(D) [83% | Correct] There are more people who are skilled at playing the guitar than there are people who are skilled at playing the banjo.
1731	(E) [7% | Contradicted] There are more people who are skilled at playing the banjo than there are people who are skilled at playing the guitar.
1732	--- BREAKDOWN
1733	## Statements
1734	Most skilled banjo are skilled guitar, but most skilled guitar are not skilled banjo.
1735	## Evaluate
1736	We know that correct answers on Inference questions want us to synthesize claims, and we only get two claims here. They have a math-y, quantified feel, so we want to think mathematically. The famous Most + Most inference that has been tested throughout the years is this: Most A's are B, Most A's are C, infer: Some B's are C / Some C's are B. But that's not what this is, since the two Most facts are about different groups. This is a new type of Most + Most inference that has now appeared 2 or 3 times on the last twenty tests. Most A's are B, Most B's are ~A, infer: There are more B's than A's. Why is that a valid inference? A little math helps. Let's say there are 9 skilled banjo players in the world. If we say that most of them are skilled at guitar, then at a minimum we have 5 people who are skilled at banjo and guitar. So now we get to the fun part: Could we also have 9 skilled guitar players in the world? If so, 5 of them are skilled at banjo and 4 aren't. But that would contradict our statement that "most skilled guitar players aren't good at banjo." What we have to remember is that the people in this world that are both skilled banjo and skilled guitar are appearing in both of these groups. When we talk about all the skilled banjo players, we'll be counting these 5 people who are good at banjo and guitar. And when we talk about all the skilled guitar players, we'll be counting these 5 people good at both in that tally as well. In order for most skilled guitar players to be bad at banjo, we need at least 11 skilled guitar players: 5 are also skilled at banjo, but the majority, the other 6, are not skilled at banjo. Here it is more graphically:
1737	```
1738	Just Banjo        Banjo and Guitar        Just Guitar
1739	    4                    5                     6
1740	```
1741	9 people are good at banjo. 11 people are good at guitar. 5 people are good at both. When we say that Most A's are B but Most B's are ~A, we're saying that people who are both A and B are a majority of Group A but a minority of Group B. In our example before, there were 5 people good at both banjo and guitar. The statements are saying that these 5 people are a majority of the skilled banjo players, so the max total for banjo would be 9. The statements are saying that these 5 people are a minority of the skilled guitar players, so the min total for guitar would be 11. if you want to see the other example of this, check out Test 80, Section 1, Q22.

1748	(D) Let's say that there are x people who are good at banjo and guitar. We are told that x > 50% of skilled banjoists. We are told that x < 50% of skilled guitarists. That means that skilled guitarists has to be a bigger number. If I say, "Whoa, I can't pay $3000 a month for that apartment. 2481	STIMULUS: Most of the mines that Moradco operates in the province of Velyena have never violated environmental regulations. Every one of the gold mines that Moradco operates throughout the world has at some time or another violated environmental regulations.
2482	STEM: Which one of the following statements follows logically from the statements above?
2483	(A) [0% | Too Strong: #1 mine operator] Moradco operates more mines in Velyena than any other company operates there.
2484	(B) [3% | Unknown Comparison / Relative vs. Absolute] The total number of gold mines that Moradco operates is larger than the total number of mines it operates in Velyena.
2485	(C) [10% | Unknown Most Group] Most of the gold mines that Moradco operates are not located in Velyena.

2488	--- BREAKDOWN
2489	## Statements
2490	```
2491	quantifier (most)
2492	Most Moradco mines           never violated
2493	in Velyena                   enviro regulations
2494	conditional / quantifier (every)
2495	Moradco               →       violated some
2496	gold mine                     enviro regulation
2497	```
2498	## Evaluate
2499	We're given a Most statement and a conditional All statement. In general, Most statements are only interesting as a starting point if you have two most claims about the same group, because then you can prove some overlap. given Most A's are B, these, Most A's are C, we can infer: B and C overlap at least once. But since there aren't two Most claims, the conditional should automatically command our attention. What does a Must Be True question do with a conditional? It either chains it together with other conditionals (there aren't any other conditionals in this two claim paragraph), or we apply this conditional to a fact that was provided, usually via the contrapositive. Does the conditional in the 2nd sentence apply to what's talked about in the 1st sentence? Is there any Overlapping Concept? Yes, both sentences are talking about whether or not something has violated environmental regulations. We know that most of M's mines in Velyena have never violated. And we know that all of M's gold mines have violated. So none of those mines we're talking about in the first sentence are gold mines.
2500	## Goal
2501	Since the mines being talked about in the first sentence clearly aren't gold mines, since they have never violated an environmental regulation, we can infer that "most of the mines that Moradco operates in the province of Velyena [are not gold mines]". Formally,
2502	```
2503	Most Moradco's           never
2504	mines in Velyena         violated
2505	+
2506	never                →   not one of
2507	violated                  Moradco's gold mines
2508	-------------------------------------------------
2509	Most Moradco's            not one of
2510	mines in Velyena          Moradco's gold mines
2511	```
2512	Like most Must Be True questions that test our ability to apply a conditional rule, you have to contrapose the rule to see how the available fact(s) triggers it.
2513	--- CHOICE NOTES
2514	(A) We have no basis for thinking Moradco operates more mines than any other company, let alone derive that they are they #1 most numerous mine operator. They could just as easily be the mining company with the fewest mines in Velyena.
2515	(B) There was no information at all about actual numbers of gold mines. Most and Every aren't numbers, they're percentages (over 50% and 100%, respectively). Just because "every" means 100% and "most" only means "over 50%" doesn't mean that there are more gold mines than Velyenan mines. There might be 100 mines in Velyena (at least 51 of which have never violated regulations). And there might be 6 gold mines that Moradco operates throughout the world (all 6 of which have violated regulations).
2516	(C) Most is a really dangerous word on Must Be True and Must Be Assumed (i.e. Necessary Assumption). We can never derive a statement about "Most X's" if the stimulus didn't provide a statement about "Most X's". There was no statement in the stimulus that said "Most gold mines from Moradco". We only had "Most Moradco mines in Velyena". This answer can be disproven with some numbers. There might be 100 mines in Velyena (at least 51 of which have never violated regulations, up to 49 of which have violated regulations). And there might be 11 gold mines that Moradco operates throughout the world (all 11 of which have violated regulations). Do we know that most of those gold mines are outside Velyena? Do at least 6 of them have to be outside Velyena? No, all 11 of them could be in Velyena and it wouldn't go against anything we were told. If there are 100 Moradco mines in Velyena, up to 49 of them could be gold mines.
2517	(D) This is what we predicted. The idea behind Inference questions is testing our ability to synthesize. Given these two claims: A is B, B is C, we derive A is C. We filter out the thing the two claims have in common and join the other two things. Given these claims: Most of Moradco's Velyena mines have violated. If have violated, then not Moradco's gold mines. We filter out the Overlapping Concept to derive: Most of Moradco's Velyena mines not Moradco's gold mines.
2518	(E) We can never derive a statement about "Most X's" if the stimulus didn't provide a statement about "Most X's". There might be 10 mines in Velyena (at least 6 of which have never violated regulations and thus are not gold). 300	## Evaluate
301	On a Must Be False question, the correct answer almost always contradicts a conditional provided in the stimulus or an available inference we can make from the stimulus. There isn't any conditional logic in this paragraph, but there is a Pivot word (however) creating tension. Usually, when there's a pivot word, the inference we can derive comes from reconciling what comes after the pivot with what came before. Here, before the pivot, we had people saying the government shouldn't take over failing banks. After the pivot. we get the author seemingly saying, "You're wrong. 1765	STIMULUS: Critic: Emily Dickinson's poetry demonstrates that meaning cannot reside entirely within a poem itself, but is always the unique result of an interaction between a reader's system of beliefs and the poem; and, of course, any two readers from different cultures or eras have radically different systems of beliefs.
1766	STEM: If the critic's statements are true, each of the following could be true except:
1767	(A) [11% | Could Be True] A reader's interpretation of a poem by Dickinson is affected by someone else's interpretation of it.
1768	(B) [77% | Correct] A modern reader and a nineteenth-century reader interpret one of Shakespeare's sonnets in the same way.
1769	(C) [1% | Could Be True] A reader's interpretation of a poem evolves over time.
1770	(D) [2% | Could Be True] Two readers from the same era arrive at different interpretations of the same poem.
1771	(E) [9% | Could Be True] A reader's enjoyment of a poem is enhanced by knowing the poet's interpretation of it.
1772	--- BREAKDOWN
1773	## Premises
1774	The critic gives us two facts: (1) meaning is always a product of *this reader* meeting *the poem*, never the poem alone; and (2) readers from different cultures or eras have radically different belief systems.
1775	## Evaluate
1776	Put those together. If meaning depends on the reader's beliefs combined with the poem, and two readers from different eras have *radically* different beliefs, then the same poem run through those two readers produces meanings shaped by radically different inputs. Those interpretations cannot be the same.
1777	The question asks which answer can't be true — i.e., which answer is ruled out by these premises.
1778	## Goal
1779	The right answer is the one where two readers from *different eras* arrive at the *same* interpretation — that's exactly what the critic's framework forbids.

1781	(A) The premises don't prevent one reader from being influenced by another. Belief systems are shaped by all sorts of things — including hearing what someone else thought of a poem. Nothing here is incompatible.
1782	(B) This is exactly what the critic's setup forbids. A modern reader and a nineteenth-century reader come from different eras, so by premise (2) they have radically different belief systems. By premise (1), each one's interpretation is shaped by their belief system meeting the poem. Different belief systems → different interpretations. They can't arrive at the same one.
1783	(C) The premises let belief systems shift over time, so the interpretation can shift with them. Nothing here is incompatible with evolving readings.
1784	(D) The premises only call out cross-era and cross-culture differences as *radical*. They don't claim readers within the same era share belief systems. So two contemporaries reaching different interpretations fits the framework just fine.
1785	(E) This is about *enjoyment*, not interpretation. The premises constrain how meaning arises — they don't say anything about what does or doesn't make reading more pleasurable. So this is fine.

Treat 11 vs 12 as a tie.\n\n**Misclassified candidates skipped (not the type named in the shortlist):**\n- MBT shortlist: PT110-S3-Q18 (Weaken: \"shows the explanation is at best incomplete\"), PT115-S2-Q10 (Strengthen: \"most support for Lorraine's conclusion\"), PT109-S4-Q24 (Weaken-style: \"conclusion would not follow if\"), PT127-S1-Q9 (Strengthen EXCEPT: \"least helpful in establishing\").\n- MBF shortlist: PT114-S1-Q10 (Strengthen / counter: \"could be used by Cora to counter\"), PT156-S2-Q5 (Flaw-style assessment of a claim). PT129-S3-Q14 (\"which situation violates the regulation\") is closer to Principle-Conform and is also transcription-damaged.\n\n---\n\n## 1. C is flagged as a one-claim answer. | Goal admits uncertainty; Evaluate is long. | N |\n\n**Alternates (strong but cut for overlap or damage):** PT127-S1-Q26 (d3 pivot, 10: good 100-theory counterexample for B, long), PT116-S3-Q21 (d4 some + all, 10: good negative-to-positive table, garbled diagram in C), PT106-S2-Q19 (d5 math, 10: good Senators analogy for the 31% trap, tables flattened, weak Goal), PT107-S4-Q23 (d4 chain + time, 10: pasted one-claim paragraph in B and C), PT143-S3-Q24 (d3 pivot sets up the trap, 10: \"woke consumerism\" aside), PT115-S4-Q6 (d3 conditional applied to a fact, 10: predicts the one inference the last sentence did not steal), PT154-S2-Q22 (d5 Most rule, 9: B, C, E pasted).\n\n### What the best MBT/MSS explanations do\n\n- **Scan for the indicator, then ask the two conditional questions out loud.** \"Can I chain it? Can I apply it to a fact (usually via the contrapositive)?\" appears almost word for word in PT103-S1-Q19 and PT155-S1-Q15, and it drives both the Evaluate and the Goal. When there is a Most claim and a conditional, the best ones say which one commands attention and why (PT145-S2-Q23: a lone Most is inert; PT122-S4-Q19).\n- **Rewrite rules into \"X requires Y\" before chaining.** PT155-S1-Q15, PT140-S3-Q17 and PT148-S3-Q17 turn \"unless\", \"only when\", \"essential\", \"cannot until\" into requirements, so the chain reads left to right and the contrapositive is easy to state in plain English.\n- **The Goal names the answer's content and its strength.** Strong: \"look for something like 'the symptoms relate to collagen'\" (PT153-S2-Q5); \"Pulham has to be higher than 38%\" (PT155-S4-Q21); \"an answer expressing this stalemate\" (PT148-S3-Q17). The better ones also predict where the traps will go (\"expect illegal negations and reversals\", PT140-S3-Q17; the list of overconfident directions in PT142-S4-Q11).\n- **Pivot endings get a named move and a worked mini-example.** \"Reconcile the Pivot\" with the Tom/Yankees example (PT142-S4-Q11), and the rebuttal reading plus \"Flip the Causal Difference-Maker\" (PT151-S3-Q14). Both warn that the right answer will be watered down.\n- **Quantity inferences are taught with head counts, not only symbols.** PT148-S1-Q22 (15 ballet lovers, minimum 17 cake lovers), PT145-S2-Q23 (100 Velyena mines vs 11 gold mines), PT155-S4-Q21 (100 + 100 students, 47%). The rule is stated so it can be memorized (\"Most A are B, Most B are not A: more B than A\"), then the numbers make it believable.\n- **Weak wording is argued for, not just noticed.** The best correct notes say how little evidence the answer needs: \"no guarantee\" needs one data point (PT102-S4-Q2, the 175/UCLA example); \"sometimes\" needs one case (PT151-S3-Q14, PT108-S2-Q17); \"can be altered\" (PT107-S1-Q6).\n- **Recurring trap names, each with a one-line test.** One-claim answer (\"feels like the first sentence on steroids\", PT148-S3-Q17; PT147-S4-Q23 C); relative vs absolute (PT107-S1-Q6 A, PT140-S3-Q17 B); only mentioned ≠ only (PT103-S1-Q19); unknown Most group (\"never conclude Most X without a Most X premise\", PT145-S2-Q23); untriggered rule (\"we don't have any knowledge triggered by...\", PT155-S1-Q15, PT149-S4-Q24).\n- **The popular trap gets the longest note and a counterexample.** PT122-S4-Q19 B (30%), PT140-S3-Q17 A and B (21% and 23%), PT146-S2-Q17 C (29%), PT148-S1-Q22 C (19%) each build a concrete scenario where the premises are true and the trap is false.\n- **When the key is not strictly provable, they say so and say why it still wins.** PT155-S1-Q15 (allowed outside vs outside), PT107-S1-Q6 (volume dial is not causal, but the stimulus supports it), PT142-S4-Q7 (correlation to \"reasonable to conclude\"). That honesty is part of teaching Most Supported.\n- **Reusable phrases:** \"pause and infer\"; \"X requires Y\"; \"remove the overlap and join the one-offs\"; \"we don't have any knowledge triggered by X\"; \"if it said ___, it would be correct\" (PT149-S4-Q24 E); \"this is the first sentence on steroids\".\n\n### What the weaker MBT candidates got wrong\n\n- **Goal hands off to the answers.** PT149-S3-Q21 (\"re-read the paragraph ... then use the answers to guide us\"), PT146-S2-Q17 and PT147-S4-Q23 admit they cannot predict. Acceptable when the stimulus is a scatter, but it costs P every time.\n- **Pasted notes instead of question-specific reasons.** PT154-S2-Q22 repeats the same Most rule in B, C and E, and C borrows B's closing sentence, which does not address oil paintings. PT127-S1-Q17 gives D (22%) and E the same one-line note. PT107-S4-Q23 pastes the one-claim paragraph into B and C.\n- **Unsupported test-taking folklore.** PT149-S3-Q21 and PT107-S1-Q21 both claim the tempting wrong answer is deliberately placed in slot (A). That is not a reasoning move and should not be copied.\n- **Diagrams flattened into unreadable lines.** PT111-S3-Q18 and PT108-S3-Q23 are strong underneath, but the conditional chains now read as word salad (\"inspired → good → sophistic → underst performances show listeners m. roots\").\n- **The popular trap gets the shortest note.** PT127-S1-Q17 D (22%): one line pasted from E. PT145-S4-Q13: A, B, C are one line each and E defers to \"the explanation for (C)\".\n\n---\n\n## 2. Must Be False (17 picks)\n\nGenuinely top-tier (11 or 12): 8 of these. The rest (9 or 10) are included to cover easy and medium difficulty and the \"most justifiably rejected\" / \"must violate\" variants, which have no stronger candidate in this shortlist. The whole family has only 42 complete explanations, so there is no deeper pool to draw from.\n\nSpread: 3 easy (d2), 1 medium (d3), 13 hard (d4-5). The family itself is skewed hard (25 of 40 complete are d4-5).\n\n| # | Slot | Diff | Sub-pattern / features | Rubric /12 | What makes it a model | Weakness | Golden? |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 1 | PT127-S1-Q25 | 5 | Contradict an inference (conditionals + Most + \"or\") | 12 | Contraposes both rules and applies them to the Most claim to derive \"most parts are not humid or not cold\", then shows A breaks it (at most 50% left). The 22% trap (D) gets the longest note: a Most \"or\" statement can be vacuously satisfied by one side (NFL/Mars), so \"no cacti anywhere\" is still possible. B, C, E each run numbers (30% + 30%, 49% + 2%). | None significant. | N |\n| 2 | PT113-S3-Q15 | 4 | Contradict a math inference (sales vs market share) | 11 | Evaluate derives two math inferences in plain terms (same sales, smaller share means the market grew) and flags the causal inference about emission standards as not a must. Goal gives the per-answer test: \"Could I infer that the opposite of this must be true?\" C and D are rejected for being unprovable in either direction. | E note is brief (profit is out of scope). | N |\n| 3 | PT115-S2-Q24 | 4 | Contradict a four-link conditional chain | 11 | Goal separates an illegal inference from a contradiction (\"A contradiction provides the trigger but denies the outcome\"). Every note then locates the answer by position in the chain (\"4th but not 3rd\", \"not 1st but 3rd\") and states the general rule for A → B → C → D. | Chain diagram is pasted into all five notes and partly garbled in transcription. | N |\n| 4 | PT153-S3-Q16 | 4 | Contradict a conditional hidden in \"some\" answers | 11 | Predicts the disguised form of the right answer (\"Jerry is a popular novelist who hates pasta and cannot empathize\": filler hides the trigger). The correct note generalizes: \"some\" is hard to contradict (you need \"none\"), \"no\" is easy (one exception), and the first two sentences supply the exception. | Evaluate's inference is thin. | N |\n| 7 | PT149-S3-Q12 | 5 | Most justifiably rejected; counterexample vs soft generalization | 11 | Teaches the core asymmetry: a categorical claim falls to one example (D: Demosthenes reads silently), a \"commonly\" or \"rarely\" claim does not (C at 17%, with the violinist analogy; E with the biological-mother example). Notes that the soft stem allows the jump from a play to real Greece. | Goal admits no prediction. | N |\n| 8 | PT121-S4-Q18 | 3 | Contradict \"only if X and Y\" | 10 | Diagrams the \"only if both\" rule as not X or not Y → not follow, and predicts both contradiction shapes.
