A (14%) is rejected by saying what rule would be needed. Fills the Refute a Conditional gap the golden set flagged. | E (17%) gets one line (\"out of scope\"). | N |\n| 10 | PT122-S2-Q21 | 4 | Causal statements; a universal answer contradicted by one case | 10 | With no conditionals, it runs one test on every choice: \"Can we contradict this? Contradicting would mean...\". B (\"invariably\") falls because small amounts of sugar both delay fatigue and help hydration. E (15%) is carefully separated: desires focus on the short term, which does not mean actions lack long-term consequences. | A, C, D are one line each (no Most facts). | N |\n| 12 | PT148-S1-Q10 | 4 | Most justifiably rejected; strong wording | 10 | Correct note explains why strong wording is easy to reject (\"no techniques\" falls to one technique: DNA analysis). Every wrong note says what the passage would have needed to say to reject it. | Goal admits no idea what to look for. | N |\n| 13 | PT102-S4-Q26 | 5 | Most justifiably rejected; spatial math | 10 | Combines the two numbers (100-130 km foraging vs a move of under 8 km) into an inference, then pictures it (a 200-260 km circle shifted 8 km is over 90% the same land). Analogy: moving eight blocks to escape citywide unemployment. | A (16%) gets one line. | N |\n| 14 | PT142-S4-Q23 | 5 | Must violate one of two rules (requirements in tension) | 10 | Goal predicts the shape before the choices: one buyer gives the top price, another gives citizen ownership, and you cannot have both. C (35%) is handled precisely: \"one of the highest offers\" is not the highest, so the government can sell to someone else. | No Evaluate step; A and B notes are short. | N |\n| 15 | PT103-S3-Q12 | 2 | Contradict a causal hypothesis | 9 | Easy model for \"the hypothesis is correct, what cannot be true\": D makes the cause impossible (\"you can't deforest an area that isn't forested\"). A, B, E are each shown to fit the story, and even to supply a cause. | Goal admits no target; T is implicit. | N |\n| 16 | PT145-S4-Q10 | 2 | Reason for rejecting; pivot + analogy | 9 | Reads the \"however\" as the author rejecting the opponents, predicts \"something like the original position\", and the correct note shows D is the opponents' assumption and how the military analogy defeats it. | Wrong-answer notes are brief. | N |\n| 17 | PT146-S3-Q22 | 2 | Contradict a universal comparison (\"every member ... any member\") | 9 | Predicts the contradiction (\"some Traditionalist at least as well qualified as some Modern member\") and shows E does it through the chairperson, who is a Modern member. D note separates \"worst Modern member\" (possible) from \"worse than a Traditionalist\" (impossible). | Short; Maples switches between his and her. | N |\n\n**Alternates:** PT111-S4-Q22 (d3 least compatible, 8: good A note, Experiment step truncated), PT107-S4-Q22 (d4 contradict an inference, 9: good unknown-Most-group rule, A is one line), PT105-S4-Q21 (d5 contradict an inference, 9: D and E pasted), PT102-S4-Q16 (d5 \"no X or Y\" vs \"no X and Y\", 9: the 39% trap B gets two sentences), PT122-S2-Q16 (d4 chain + Most, 9: B, C, E pasted), PT114-S4-Q25 (d4 compatible except, causal, 9: typo in D).\n\n### What the best MBF explanations do\n\n- **Open Evaluate with the two-pattern rule, then look for each.** \"The correct answer almost always contradicts a conditional or contradicts an inference we can make by combining claims\" (PT127-S1-Q25, PT113-S3-Q15, PT147-S1-Q20). Then they actually derive the inference or state the contradiction shape, rather than stopping at the rule.\n- **Define a contradiction precisely and separate it from a bad inference.** \"Trigger, but don't deliver\" (PT153-S3-Q16); \"a contradiction provides the trigger but denies the outcome; reversals and negations are illegal but not contradictions\" (PT115-S2-Q24); \"Bad Inference ≠ contradiction\" (PT124-S2-Q10). This is the single most important transfer line for the family.\n- **Every wrong-answer note runs the same test: what would we need to have read to contradict this?** PT110-S3-Q22, PT121-S4-Q18, PT148-S1-Q10 and PT122-S2-Q21 phrase it as \"To contradict this, we would need to have read that ___. We never heard that.\" PT113-S3-Q15 and PT117-S4-Q18 phrase it as \"Can I prove the opposite?\" Either way, it replaces verdict-only \"out of scope\" notes.\n- **Teach the strength asymmetry.** Weak claims (\"some\", \"commonly\", \"could\") are hard to contradict; strong claims (\"no\", \"never\", \"invariably\", \"no techniques\") fall to one counterexample. PT110-S3-Q22 states it, PT149-S3-Q12 and PT148-S1-Q10 apply it to soft stems, PT122-S2-Q21 applies it to a causal passage.\n- **Predict the disguise.** PT153-S3-Q16 predicts filler around the trigger (\"hates pasta\"); PT147-S1-Q20 says D matches the prediction \"but they disguised it well\"; PT142-S4-Q23 predicts two requirements in tension before reading the choices.\n- **Locate answers by position on a chain.** PT115-S2-Q24: \"something is the 1st idea but not the 3rd\" contradicts; \"the 4th but not the 3rd\" does not. That turns five choices into one quick check.\n- **Run numbers on quantified \"or\" and Most claims.** PT127-S1-Q25 (30% + 30% non-overlapping; 49% + 2%) and PT113-S3-Q15 ($1 million at 20% vs 13%) make the math visible, and PT127-S1-Q25's NFL/Mars example shows why a Most \"or\" claim can be satisfied by one side alone.\n- **Tell the student what one word would make a wrong answer right.** PT153-S3-Q16 B (\"if it said some popular novelists\"), PT105-S4-Q21 A. This shows exactly where the line is.\n- **Reusable phrases:** \"trigger, but don't deliver\"; \"can't prove it ≠ can disprove it\" (PT102-S4-Q15); \"we can't contradict something we never talked about\"; \"to contradict this, we would need to know ___\"; \"could I infer that the opposite must be true?\".\n\n### What the weaker MBF candidates got wrong\n\n- **Verdict-only notes, especially for \"out of scope\" answers.** PT134-S2-Q18 (notes of one sentence each), PT129-S2-Q23, PT102-S4-Q15 (A to D are one or two lines). An out-of-scope note is only useful when it says what contradiction would have been needed.\n- **Pasted notes.** PT152-S2-Q24 D and E are identical (and both wrong, below); PT122-S2-Q16 B, C, E share one note; PT105-S4-Q21 D and E share one note; PT108-S3-Q18 opens A, B, C, E with the same boilerplate.\n- **Slips that invert the logic of a note.** PT152-S2-Q24 says \"we can contradict this\" where it means \"can't\"; PT116-S3-Q17's Goal lists a wrong second contradiction pattern; PT122-S4-Q5's Evaluate treats a Most claim as All. In a family built on the difference between can't-prove and can-disprove, these are serious.\n- **Popular trap under-served.** PT102-S4-Q16 B (39%) gets two sentences; PT147-S1-Q20 E (17%) and PT102-S4-Q26 A (16%) get one line.\n- **Different register.** PT112-S4-Q7 uses heavy em-dashes and a voice unlike the rest of the corpus (\"i.e., which answer is ruled out\"); accurate, but not a style model.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Fidelity problems found (all candidates read, picked or not)\n\n### Must Be True / Most Supported\n- **PT145-S2-Q23** (picked, golden): D note says \"Most of Moradco's Velyena mines have violated\" where it means \"have never violated\".\n- **PT145-S4-Q13**: E note refers the student to \"the explanation for (C)\"; it means (D).\n- **PT111-S3-Q18**: every conditional chain is flattened onto one line and unreadable; the A contrapositive (\"not → not → not → not 4th 3rd 2nd 1st\") is garbled. (transcription)\n- **PT108-S3-Q23**: chains flattened in Statements and in the B and E notes; the A note never actually says why A is wrong. (transcription)\n- **PT140-S3-Q17** (picked): Statements and Evaluate diagrams flattened; the choice-note diagrams survive. (transcription)\n- **PT106-S2-Q19**: worked-number tables collapsed with run-together words (\"150pieces of mail 100 correctly addressed50incorrect\").
